ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u1	Good morning. Let's start the session. The session is open. He justified the non-attendance of Mrs. Cristina Romero Fernández. Let's start with the legislative texts. Debates and votes by Ditame, drawn up by Commission 4th, Education and Culture, on the draft law on the cultural heritage of Galicia
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u2	The amendments of the Joint Parliamentary Group, of the Parliamentary Group of the Alternative Galga de Left, of the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego and of the Socialist Parliamentary Group are kept for the plenary. The president of Commission 4th, Education and Culture, Mr Antonio Mouriño, spoke for the presentation of the commission's dictation.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u3	Thank you, President. Mr Councillor for Culture, Education and Vocational Education, ladies and gentlemen, as chairman of the Fourth Committee on Education and Culture, I should like to present to this plenary the Ditam of the draft law on Galician cultural heritage. The project was entered into Parliament's Register on December 4, 2015. The Bureau of Parliament, at its meeting on 9 December 2015, issued its publication in the Official Bulletin of the Parliament of Galicia and the opening of the deadline for the submission of amendments, which ended on 29 December for amendments to the whole and on 29 January 2016 for amendments to the articulated. Even so, listening to the Speaker's Board, he agreed to the 4th Commission on Education and Culture. The amendments to the whole, qualified by the Bureau of the committee at the meeting on 27 January 2016 and published in the Official Bulletin of the Parliament of Galicia No. 591, of 28 January 2016, were a total of three: one from the Joint Parliamentary Group, one from the Parliamentary Group of the Left Gallaga Alternative and one from the Parliamentary Group of the Galician Nationalist Block. They were all rejected at the plenary meeting on 9 February 2016. The amendments to the joint tabled and qualified by the Bureau of the Committee at its meeting on 10 February 2016 and published in the Official Bulletin of the Parliament of Galicia No. 599 of 11 February 2016 were a total of 633: 204 of the Joint Parliamentary Group, 201 of the Parliamentary Group of the Galga de Left, 176 of the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block of Galicia, 50 of the Parliamentary Group of the Socialists of Galicia and 2 of the People's Parliamentary Group of Galicia. At its meeting on 25 April 2014, Commission 4th on Education and Culture appointed the following speakers: For the Popular Parliamentary Group, Fr. Agustín Baamonde Díaz, holder, and Mr. Cesar Manuel Fernández Gil, alternate. For the Parliamentary Group of Socialists of Galicia, Fr Emilio Vázquez Blanco, holder, and Ms Maria Concepción Burgo López, alternate. For the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego, Ms. Ana Belén Pontón Mondelo, holder, and Mr. Cosme Eladio Pombo Rodríguez, alternate. For the Parliamentary Group of the Alternative Glaga de Left, Mr José Javier Ron Fernández, holder, and Mr Ramón Vázquez Díaz, alternate. And by the Joint Parliamentary Group, Mrs. Maria Consuelo Martínez García, headline, and Mrs. Monica Fernández Rodríguez, alternate. The inaugural session of the presentation took place on March 2, 2016, where the methodology and the working calendar were established. The following meetings were held on 21, 28, 30 and 31 March and 6 and 8 April 2016. The proposed amendments and the project text were analysed. The last meeting was held on 8 April 2016 and the report was finalized; it was discussed and voted on at the 4th meeting of the Committee on 14 April, at which all the recommendations made by the presentation in its report were collected. The articulated text of the ditam is structured into 141 articles, distributed in a preliminary title and 10 titles. Title I establishes the classification of the assets of Galician cultural heritage. It defines the protection environment and regulates the buffer zone. This title also creates the Census of Cultural Heritage. Title II regulates the generic regime for the protection and conservation of Galician cultural heritage. In Title III, the details regarding the specific protection scheme for declared goods of cultural interest are inserted. Title IV establishes the specific regime for the protection of goods of the Galician Cultural Heritage Catalogue. Title V includes the peculiarities of the intangible heritage. Title VI collects and regulates the Santiago Road Protection Scheme. In the following title, Title VII sets out the different specific heritages which are part of Galicia's cultural heritage. Title VIII establishes the network of museums and creates the network and the Galician Museum System. Title IX regulates the promotion of Galicia's cultural heritage, establishing the essential measures for its provision and favoring its conservation. Finally, title X regulates the inspector activity and the sanctioning regime. The final part of the text includes a series of forecasts aimed at ordering and defining commitments for regulatory development and putting at the disposal of citizenship and advertising instruments for cultural heritage assets, as well as establishing the necessary transitional rules and delimiting the entry into force of the various obligations raised in this document. So far a summary of the work assigned to Commission 4th, which I am presiding over. It is up to me today to submit your Ditami to your consideration of the plenary. Nothing more and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u4	Thank you, Mr. Mouriño. The turn of parliamentary groups to set their position on the dictatorship, and if so, to defend the amendments maintained. For the Joint Parliamentary Group, Mrs Martínez.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u5	Thank you, President. Good morning to all and to all. We kept the amendments. We presented 203 amendments, of which 16 were accepted and traded 7; so we kept the remaining 179 amendments. I can't defend each of them, so I'll group them according to their motives and explain some of them. There are amendments related to the object to be protected, such as Amendments 13, 15, 16 and 17 to Article 9, to include as objects to protect biodiversity, oak trees, strawberries and the Atlantic Mountains, which coincide with Amendment 26 to Article 10, which seeks cataloguing as an asset of cultural interest from the indigenous landscapes. We also maintain, in relation to the object to be protected, amendments 23 to 25 to article 10, which aim to take into account, in addition to historical and architectural interest, social interest, in which, for example, washing machines would be included. This amendment is supplemented by Amendments Nos 142 and 144 to Article 92, to apply to washing machines the protection that applies to horreros, cruises and pockets of souls. We consider the washing machines, even if they do not take into account the text of the bill, to tell the story, basically, of the Greek women. The laundry was where they met and where they articulated many of the actions they carried out in history. Also amendments 137 to 141 to Article 91, which add as an ethnological heritage the docks, beacons, maritime signs, useful marines or popular songs. All traditional agricultural and fisheries equipment should be part of the cultural heritage. Also amendment 155 to article 10, to define the bibliographic heritage that remains undefined. We also present and maintain amendments related to the participation, rights and duties of the citizen, such as Amendment 4 to Article 5, which includes the obligation of any citizen to communicate the danger of deterioration or destruction of property, as well as the obligation of the Administration to verify the truthfulness of the complaint. It cannot be allowed, and the law should lay down the obligation that any citizen should inform the Administration of the danger of deterioration or destruction of the property and the obligation of the Administration itself to act immediately in response to these complaints, because we believe that it is the only way to prevent the deterioration of the property. Amendment 53 to Articles 23, 55 and 56 to Articles 25 and 62 to Article 29 also requests that information on property ownership be made public when belonging to a company, foundation, organization or religious entity. We understand that some data is protected; data from owners who cannot be made public, but when this data is from societies, organizations or religious entities should be publicized. We also maintain Amendment No 71 to Article 34, which calls for the public exposure of all plans, programmes and projects that may affect cultural heritage, and which should be subject to a period of public information. Any action that may be carried out and may affect the assets should have a period of public exposure so that persons can pronounce themselves on that impact. We also keep amendments related to the Catholic Church. We can say that this law, as its text is contemplated, we could call it a confessional law. We therefore submit and maintain Amendment 5 to Article 6, which obliges the Union to draw up a relationship of cultural heritage on behalf of the Catholic Church and to claim the return – of the habeles – of the properties duly inscribed on its name. In Galicia there is no relation of the properties in the name of the Church, in which year they were dedicated to the Church, and whether that adjudication is legitimate or not. So it's about establishing that relationship and claiming those properties that are legitimately ascribed and registered in the name of the Catholic Church. We also maintain Amendment No 6 to Article 7, which eliminates the Joint Joint Committee as an advisory and advisory body in the field of cultural heritage, and Amendment No 14 to Article 9, which seeks to limit social, ritual, ceremonial or holiday uses as an asset. And we add that they don't have a confessional character. Ceremonies, rituals or religious acts cannot be part of the cultural heritage, but they are, in any case, the heritage of each religious confession. Amendments also related to advisory and advisory bodies, such as Amendment 11 to Article 7, to include the Technical Committee of Museums among the advisory bodies. We do not understand why they refuse to accept this amendment when, until now, it was part of this committee, the Technical Committee of Museums. Amendment 12 to Article 7 too, so that all the real schools and universities in Greece are part of the consultative bodies. One thing we don't understand is why, for example, the Royal Language Academy is not part of the consultative bodies, when the language is part of the unimportant heritage of Greek. Amendment 79 to Article 44, which claims that in order to eliminate some construction of past periods of good time, there must be a favourable report from the advisory bodies. Article 44 states that the administrative silence would allow for the destruction of assets, and we believe that, in any case, we can never, if there is no favourable report from the advisory bodies, eliminate or destroy assets. Amendment 91 to Article 52 also establishes the need for a good visa of the advisory bodies for the transfer of immovable property which are part of the cultural heritage. We speak not of furniture, but of immovable property; therefore, of destroying a construction and moving it from place to place, and for that we find it important that consultative bodies act, because in every law the consultative bodies are called but they are not given any role to those advisory bodies, and then they do not act on any of the decisions to be made, and most of them are settled by administrative silence. We also keep amendments related to the protection of heritage, since the bill is excessively deprotective. So, we keep Amendment 34 to Article 14 to correct the text of the bill, since it eliminates any duty to protect, maintain and preserve assets that form part of the Census of Galician Cultural Heritage. We understand that any asset that is part of the Census, although not part of the Catalogue, should – in principle and preventively – be subject to protection, maintenance and conservation. We also maintain Amendment 35 to Article 14, which provides as appropriate the authorization of the competent council in the field of heritage to act on any property included in the Census of Cultural Heritage, and Amendment 128 to Article 85, to prohibit, without exclusion, the destruction of the assets of the artistic heritage. It's incomprehensible that a law that, in theory, is born to protect heritage establishes the permit for its destruction; it's the first time. Laws are to protect, and here's a law that says that because they have no social interest, because they don't have certain very subjective issues – which also doesn't mark who has to define those issues; you don't know who would define them – if you can destroy heritage or goods that are included within the Cultural Heritage Census. We also keep amendments related to the administrative silence to change their meaning, since we understand that this is employed against the interest of safeguarding the heritage. So, we maintain Amendment 36 to Article 16, where the administrative silence dismisses the declaration of good. In any case, administrative silence can never deprotect good. If the administrative silence dismisses the declaration of good it is saying that that good can be destroyed, modified and abandoned. So it's a risk of destroying something that has value just because you don't act as long as you've established in your own law. Amendment 58 to article 26 and Amendment 60 to article 28, where administrative silence dismisses the inclusion of a property in the Catalogue of Galician Cultural Heritage. Once again, the administrative silence serves to deprotect and, therefore, to authorize the destruction of the heritage. We therefore believe that administrative silence should in any event, if not limited, prolong the conservation and protection of good. We maintain Amendment No 72 to Article 34, where administrative silence allows municipalities to operate in areas with cultural goods to be preserved without the binding report of the competent council, and of course without a report of the advisory and advisory bodies. In other words, it can be destroyed by works in the municipal heritage simply by leaving the Union itself. And Amendment 152 to Article 108, which allows the destruction of assets that are part of the scientific and technical heritage without the authorization of the competent councillor. We can therefore state that the administrative silence in this law is directed in all cases to deprotect the heritage and thus allow its destruction or modification. We also maintain amendments related to the actions of the Welsh Administration, such as Amendment 70 to Article 33, which adds the duty of protecting, preserving and enriching the Greek cultural heritage by the Administration. The incredible thing is that the board doesn't accept in a law that its function is to protect, preserve and enrich the heritage, I don't know if it's because the object of the board is another. We also maintain Amendment 74 to Article 35, which adds the signature of protocols between Union and municipalities to ensure the protection and conservation of cultural heritage. Nor do we understand why they refuse to establish these heritage conservation protocols. And Amendment No 77 to Article 43 on the assumption of political responsibilities for damage and loss of cultural heritage, because all of this administrative silence and all of the measures of deprotection since then include sanctions for people, but there is no sanction or assumption of political responsibilities for the abandonment of functions and abandonment of heritage. We also maintain Amendment 86 to Article 47, which adds a new article that regulates the obtaining of licenses to perform works in the heritage. And Amendment No 90 to Article 50, which states that the listed assets owned by the Autonomous Community are irrevocable, unbending and inalienable; something which has not been accepted by the Government, by the People's Party. We maintain the amendments in relation to immaterial heritage, such as Amendment No 107, which adds a new article defining immaterial cultural heritage, which is not defined in the law; Amendment No 108 to Article 168, which obliges the Union to ensure the use of toponyms in all areas, institutions and entities of the Administration – especially because it does not have to listen again to Rajoy who has been in "Sanjenjo" and has passed through Ghrove, those things that Mr. Rajoy tells us on television. Also Amendment 109 to Article 69, so that Galician Radio-Television broadcasts programs that value intangible cultural heritage. The People's Party is again denied using Galician Radio-Television as a cultural audiovisual medium that transmits the culture and values of our country. We also maintain amendments 118 and 119, which add two new articles in relation to the Greek language as an immaterial heritage and urgent measures with regard to it. And Amendment 158 to Article 122, to encourage in compulsory education the normalized use of Greek and knowledge of Galician heritage, history and culture. This is indeed a scandal, that the People's Party, the party that supports the Government, refuses to promote in teaching the normalized use of Greek and the knowledge of Galician heritage, history and culture. They'll explain why you refuse. Many amendments also aim to convert multiple non-definitions of actions that may or may not be brought to an end. These definitions appear in Amendments 43, 44, 82, 83, 85, 96, 110, 113, 114, 133 and 146. They all talk about things that can be done, but they don't establish when they can, under what conditions, who can... so what they say is they don't do things. We maintain the amendments related to the system of shortcomings and sanctions, which we consider to be excessively benevolent, and we maintain all the amendments to Articles 129 to 131 and 144, related to the qualification of shortcomings, which I consider to be scarce coercive. There are really very serious shortcomings that are classified as mild; serious, very serious faults there are few; and there are shortcomings that are regarded as light and which, however, destroy the heritage. So our amendments are intended to establish a greater gravity for the shortcomings; greater protection of the heritage and greater responsibility for those who are responsible for the destruction of the heritage and for the political responsibilities of those who allow these actions. Finally, we will vote against this bill because, rather than protect, protect cultural heritage and disregard immaterial cultural heritage, hampering the knowledge of our culture and our language by our youth and through school, and by not using this law to enhance and protect material and intangible cultural heritage and not protect the biodiversity and the Greek landscapes. Nothing else. Thank you very much.

So we present 201 amendments, and the great generosity of the People's Party has accepted 14, and 2 transactions. So, as we see, it is clear that the will of dialogue was quite small, quite non-existent. We accept from the Gaga de Left Alternative that, logically, our ideological stance with respect to cultural heritage is totally different from what the People's Party defends on the right, which, as we said, wants to convert our heritage into a good of tourist interest, into a good of commercial interest, and we say that culture is not a commodity. So from the first amendment, from Amendments 1 and 2 to the title of Article 1, we propose to radically change the definition of the People's Party of Culture, of cultural heritage. We propose a broad definition, involving, social, inclusive, participatory, that deepens the maximum responsibility of the Galician Union as public administration in ensuring the protection, protection, restoration, conservation, safeguarding, research, dissemination and dissemination of our historical, cultural and natural heritage. And we also record from there and until the end of the 201 amendments the express recognition of the right of citizenship to participate in decision-making, in their accessibility, in the knowledge and enjoyment of the cultural and cultural heritage, incorporating the dimension of cultural heritage into the social and ecological good that we have to transmit. We often speak of a hidden and silent cultural wealth that requires – as the specialist in Theory of Cultural Heritage González Varas says – "of an act of will to be exhumed from the noises of forgetfulness". And you are, of course, very comfortable leaving it in forgetfulness, because so the market and those market laws can do what they want with our historical, cultural and natural heritage; at the bottom, with our history, with our memory. Heritage allows us to understand what we are, but we must not be confused, we cannot become synonymous with culture and cultural heritage. Cultural heritage is a critical selection of significant witnesses, both material and immaterial, cultural and natural, of our past; a critical selection that depends on social, ideological and intellectual valuations, which speaks of our relationship from now on with our past. So as a selection, this cannot be the result of an evading process of participation, as is the case today, it needs the broadest participation in its definition and conformation. This fact is fundamental to place cultural heritage in its context, in its community, overcoming its own approaches of the 19th century which revolved around monumentalism and the museum as dominant institutions and which send us to an elite, exclusive and made from above. It will not be necessary to remember that in 1954 the Hague Convention introduced the notion of cultural good to overcome precisely that decimononic approach, as it seeks to be more inclusive and covers more aspects that go beyond the materiality of monumentalism. The selection with the participation of the whole community in its broadest sense, that's what we defend in our amendments, because that's what will allow a cultural object to become cultural good. This selection can only reach a good port if there is a historical work, and this fact only makes sense in a society which has institutionalized historical knowledge in education and which is also present in the social media. Well, we can clearly say that education is not present, and through the exercise of amendments that you have refused, because it will not be present. And if he's not present in education, we can hardly raise awareness about the importance of our past; our past in this present, to explain precisely and not make the mistakes we're making. That's why the resulting text is directed and paternalist, at the same time as exclusive, and analogous to the 19th century is thought of from above. From that point on, our amending concern was aimed at incorporating participation at all times from Amendment 4 to Article 1, to guarantee the right of access to culture and to the participation of citizens and local communities. You refuse that. And who's getting those churches out of the mud and forgetfulness? Is it the Catholic Church itself? Is that the Public Service? No, it's the local communities that try to keep them alive. And you even deny that participation and deny that right. In Amendments 22, 23 and 25 to Article 7, of institutional academic participation, we have made a broad commitment to incorporate all technical commissions that are appropriate from the point of view of academic disciplines and all bodies that are dedicated to studying, working and deepening our historical, cultural and natural heritage in all its dimensions. Amendment 77 to Article 36(1) defines access, and we ask once again for the legislator's firm will to have the competent public administration lay down the appropriate measures to allow such knowledge, access and enjoyment, because you refuse. And that is why I am going to say that one of the amendments that, of course, produces more annoyance to us than to accept it is Amendment 201, where we want to add a new provision and make us note and we ask that within two years of the entry into force of this law, in cooperation with the representative entities of functional diversity in Galicia, a map of the main goods of cultural interests and catalogued to be carried out in them the relevant actions of location, signalectics, pedestrian itineraries, manageability, information, understandable and friendly, to comply with universal accessibility rules and that no one is excluded from the knowledge and enjoyment of our historical, cultural and natural heritage. That's discrimination for us. And you're still mostly stranded to do it. Amendment 106 to Article 43(3) speaks of intervention and final memory projects. We ask people to participate with knowledge of the scientific and professional disciplines involved in research, conservation, restoration or rehabilitation. You accepted only one. We don't understand why you don't accept this dialogue of two meetings; a dialogue that we're going to keep here to accept the full participation of professionals in the sector, so that they won't be aggravated by this famous tourism decree that obliges them to qualify. It's an absolute sense that you don't act that way. But also imperatively, a law has to do with our historical, cultural and natural heritage, it has to dialogue with science; this aspect, of course, that is absent in the Council of Galicia, because you have to remember that I am able to overcome the systematic and scientific differences to convert all cold mineral-medicine water into water waters by mere tourist and mercantil claim, as you are doing with the goods of cultural interest, about those that hang on this bill to conversion into goods of tourist and commercial interest. That's the purpose of this bill. There is also Article 38, so nothing can prevent free market initiatives. But in the free market against cultural heritage, it always loses the cultural heritage and loses our identity as a people. That's why we cannot defend this absolute lack of science, of a scientific nature, which you demonstrate in chapter I of Title I. It is true that there may be differences and nuances, but there are many aspects that are left out, which are seriously degraded from the scientific point of view and from the point of view of bureaucratic complexity. You make everything more bureaucratically complex. They turn safe aspects into legally insecure and maintain conceptual and epistemic confusions that are serious from a point of view, to our understanding, academic. ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u8	Thank you, Mr. Ron. For the Parliamentary Group of the Nationalist Block Galego has the floor to Mrs. Pontón Mondelo.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u9	Thank you very much, Madam President. Good morning. I would like, first of all, to begin with thanks: first of all, thanks to the people who follow this debate, who are interested in what we are debating in this Parliament about this Cultural Heritage Act; thanks also to all the people and all the entities who have contributed to us, because I want to stress that a good part of the amendments that we are presenting takes up the feeling of cultural professionals, of people who are working on the heritage and who see the great effects that this legislation has and the problems that it will create in the future; and thirdly also a thanks both to the Juridical Services of the House and to all the members of the presentation, because I have to recognise that they have facilitated, at least, the work in placement, although at the same time I clearly also say that their results are clearly unsatisfactory from the point of view of the Nationalist Galego. And why are these results unsatisfactory? Because really the People's Party is once again making a deaf ear policy, of wanting to blind us to reality, by alone approving a new rule that actually worsens legislation on heritage; a new standard that really collects is a fossilized conception of what our heritage is and which, in addition, represents, therefore, a missed opportunity for our country. We understand that the problem of deprotection that has the heritage in Galicia is not located in the legislation, that we even have a legislation that, if it had developed in a clear way, with resources, with technical support from the Administration, has very good things. We did not deny the possibility of adapting legislation to new times, we did not deny the possibility of having a new heritage law that really understood that heritage is a social value, that it is a cultural value and that it is also a value, therefore, of cohesion and development in our country. Unfortunately, we have a rule that is a step backwards in the deprotection of heritage and that is not born of consensus. I want to highlight this for one thing, because the legislation that Galicia had was a unanimous legislation passed in this House. And what we regret is that the PP, without making a critical analysis of what are the problems of deprotection of heritage, makes a new law that clearly is a step back in relation to our country. So, since the Galician Nationalist Block, we not only announced our vote against this law because it deprotects and discredits legislation in the field of heritage protection, but we also made a commitment, and it is necessary that in the next legislation we should repeal this law and we should bring about a new law of cultural heritage that is really a suitable law at times, to a modern conception of heritage management and that it is in line with the sector, that it is financially endowed, because you continue in the policy-fiction saying that you can protect the wealth without resources, and once again we denounce that this law is accompanied by a memory that is a shame, because it tells us that with 400,000 euros will apply this law annually. How are we going to apply a new law that means a step forward in protecting the heritage if it has no resources? Let's explain it because we really think it's an unsolved puzzle. And also, of course, we believe it is important to bet on the recovery of our country's heritage as an element of self-esteem, as an element of cohesion, as an element of development, and we believe that you have a blindness there that is not only causing the destruction of our country's heritage, but limiting the possibilities of economic development. We also regret that most of the amendments we tabled to this law were not accepted. They were partial amendments which, of course, cannot change what is a structure and a conception which, from our point of view, is not that which at this time needs the heritage of our country, but, in any case, we want to emphasize that the most relevant thing that has been approved of us is precisely in Article 14: that this scandalous reference is deleted to the fact that the fact that it is included in the Census of Cultural Heritage does not mean its physical preservation. Obviously, you realize it's an aberration that had appeared that the fact that a good person was in the Census did not imply its preservation. Removing that reference to us is something positive, although, as we say, it does not have enough substance to this matter, so that we can modify or there is a substantial change to this law. It's a law that, as we have already said, undermines legislation on heritage, which actually nullifies the powers of the Galician Union. You're always in that policy of wanting to be irresponsible and, therefore, there's an important step back in the protection of heritage, which is going to be greater discretionality and legal insecurity and even confusion. We believe it's a mistake to eliminate inventories, because the fact that there were three categories in the area of Greek culture responds to the fact that the reality of our heritage is not homologable to another. We have a very rich heritage, very extensive. We're talking here about harrows, washing machines, pockets of souls, many elements of our traditional heritage that need protection, but without the rigidity of a good of cultural interest or a good that is catalogued, and you in that area, as we say, what they do is take a step back. We continue to denounce that social participation in heritage management is not incorporated. We believe that we should eliminate the privileges that the Catholic Church continues to have in this legislation, and therefore a regime specifically envisaged for the protection of that heritage, which, on the other hand, seeing what is happening in this country, it is clear that it has failed, because it does not obtain better conservation, recovery and public access to the assets that are currently held by the Catholic Church, and also because we believe that there are problems, even from a technical point of view, in the concepts that are collected in this law that will generate many problems. One of them is to date heritage or consideration of heritage from a historical point of view rather than from its social value and cultural value. And even that step that you take in this law is deprotecting the heritage that today was already protected, or even in terms of, for example, underwater heritage, they are legislating against what the underwater heritage protection conventions just referred to as referendums say. I mean, this is a law that creates legal insecurity, that technically has a lot of inaccuracies and that, as we say, it's not useful to solve our country's problems. We are very surprised that some of the amendments we tabled were not collected, I say, because many are technical issues that happen to us from people who are working in the heritage field and who warn of problems by the inaccurate definitions that are put into the law, and others have to do with even aspects that were already collected in the legislation and that years ago they seemed to be important to us. We are surprised that the People's Party, which is so filled with the words of the internationalization of culture, does not accept an amendment in which we speak of the external projection of the Greek cultural heritage, even when that amendment was already collected in the current legislation and therefore marked an element in which we had agreed years ago. Today the People's Party is against collecting the external projection of the Greek cultural heritage. We also do not understand why the rights and duties of citizenship are being curtailed in this law, and in particular, we seem to be relevant to us, and we believe it is important that in the legislation it is recognized the duty that all people should participate in the protection of their heritage and in the de facto denunciation of aggressions to their cultural heritage, because we also know that, without that citizenship alert network, with the technical means of Union, it is impossible to detect the cases of destruction of our heritage. So why is the People's Party against the collection of an article – which was also in the Law on the Vigilant Cultural Heritage – in which it is said that when people observe the danger of destruction or deterioration of an integral property of the cultural heritage, they must put it in the knowledge of the Administration or of justice itself? Why is it being eliminated as a citizen duty and therefore as a common element to the protection and detection of such cases? You should explain this to us. We would also like to be explained why they continue to maintain privileges for the Catholic Church, establishing a specific regime in which even the Catholic Church does not demand the same from the rest of the owners and owners of cultural interests, property in our country. It would be important if we were told the cause. We think there's also one, as I said, need to recover the category of inventory. We don't understand why you're betting on a buffer zone, when what you're doing is limiting the protection that already exists and complicating the protection that already existed with the protective contours of cultural interests. We understood that this law was an opportunity to agitate and debureautize declarations of cultural interests in our country. To declare a good of cultural interest, the average processing of reports is very long. Why don't we use this law to make that processing more agile? Because, indeed, we have a very rich heritage, we have figures that the logic would be that it would not take an eternity to process them from a bureaucratic point of view. So we said: to speed up the statement, that it is only necessary for a report to be able to declare a good of cultural interest and that, in case it is not stated, we do not have to wait three years to start that file again; therefore, let us facilitate protection and discredit administrative procedures. But here you keep all this, that's right, with a question you haven't been able to explain to us yet, and we're very concerned about access, about the conditions for public visit to declared assets of cultural interest. An additional provision appears here that seems to have been three years of moratorium so that visits to goods of cultural interest are not mandatory. You make an additional provision in which you give three years to communicate to the board of directors the conditions and calendars of your public visit. I mean, right now, if you don't communicate, you're three years old when cultural interests won't be able to be visible. We'd like to know why they put this additional provision when we're really not legislating ex novo; that is, this figure does not appear in a new way in this law, but that was already part of the legal body of the previous Cultural Heritage Act. Let us explain why this additional provision appears which, of course, as we say, is a step back, and even gives the impression that for three years in all cultural interests, if it is not communicated to the Board what the conditions and the calendar are, there should not be any public visits. It seems very serious to us, and we ask that our Amendment 175 be adopted today, which, in any case, sets a more reasonable deadline than a three-year deadline. We would also like them to correct, as I said, some inaccuracies or questions that are very surprising from the point of view of lack of rigor or how we were pointing out that there are problems, because we are cataloging and misdefining what is our own heritage. For example, as far as defensive architecture is concerned, you only refer to castles, defensive towers and urban surrounding walls. We asked ourselves: And a chest, the walls of a castle, aren't they a defensive structure? Because if you make these kind of definitions that clearly soon create technical problems, because they set out definitions that have nothing to do with reality. And I didn't want to finish -- we could comment on many more amendments -- without referring to two that seem important to us. We're making a proposal here to create archaeologists; that is, spaces where we can accumulate, study, conserve, preserve and investigate our archaeological heritage. They know there's a very serious problem because of the lack of space and the lack of places specifically designed for this. We don't know why we don't want to move forward in this field. And so we're also surprised that we don't want to give a solution to a problem that has been created in recent months and that they have all those people who are working on the heritage; those who are working because they're doing excavations in the archaeological heritage, but also all those people who want to do an activity related to putting into value of the archaeological heritage and also job creation, the professionals who are working in the field of dissemination, display and conservation of our cultural heritage. We have a legislation today that creates aberrations such as that a person who has been responsible for an archaeological excavation cannot make a guided visit to teach this good to anyone, has to take a technician who has a tourist guide skills. It seems very good to us that the figure of the tourist guide is regulated and that this therefore has a professionalization in the Italian field. Now, what's a mistake is to consider that all the heritage is tourism and it's a mistake to consider that culture professionals can't continue to develop the work they're doing. We have an amendment, which is our Amendment No 157, which collects the proposal they moved...

ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u15	Thank you, President. From the Socialist Group we have from the very first moment that this law was an unnecessary law. The Group of the People's Party and the Government of the United Nations always endorses the initiatives put forward since the opposition in this field, as they did in the previous plenary session. And at the same time they announce this bill as if it had solved the problem of abandoning our cultural assets. By not adding that the management of the heritage and the actions of protection, conservation, restoration, rehabilitation and worth it constitute an important niche of employment for our country. And, curiously, the departure devoted to the protection of artistic, historical and cultural heritage increased from 22,168,000 euros to 12,834,000, reducing each year in proportion to the abandonment of cultural goods. And we see how in the budgets of the Union for this year – those that are supposed to be expansive – still decrease by 6% with respect to 2015 the game devoted to the protection and promotion of historical, artistic and cultural heritage. In addition to the scarce budget allocated to this matter, the Board practically only invests in the preservation of the heritage goods of the Catholic Church, clearly leaving other civic works of relevance. And it must also be said that the owners of BIC-declared goods are not being demanded, in many cases restored with public subsidies, which enable the days and hours of visit, as the legislation determines. That is why the Socialist Group maintains that the problem of heritage in Galicia is not a normative problem. We believe that the problem is in politics, and if this doesn't change, it will do nothing to pass a new law. Moreover, we maintain that the applicable law is a perfectly valid law, no consensus, an important fact, because this law is not. And what we need is for normative development to cover the deficiencies and that some of the issues that are going to fail, but of course what we have to do is that it was unnecessary to make a new law; and we maintain that the problem of heritage in Galicia is not a legal problem, but it is a political problem, and what we need is political will and commitment. And let me remind you once again: for this government culture is not a priority, and this is clear by the level of investment, because since 2009 the budget in culture has been reduced by 56%. But in addition, the percentage allocated to culture over the total in 2009 was 1.29% and now is 0.6% – less than half – and these are also objective data. As I said, this law was not necessary because the previous law was made with a broad consensus, which we think is very important for a law like the one that regulates our heritage. And, of course, despite the insistence on the participative nature itself that he intended to give to this law, it is obvious that he does not count on the participation and the search for consensus that has been achieved in the previous law; it is enough to point out as an example that the pre-project claim period was nothing less than in the past summer, from then on the worst possible date when he has an interest in involving the interested and affected groups; not to mention the summons he has made to the different opposition groups by presenting us with a power-point of the pre-project law when days before the Board of Directors had already approved the bill we are debating here today. From the Socialist Group we presented a series of amendments that they were looking for to correct different aspects of the law that concern us and that we also believe that they constitute a clear setback in the field of heritage. It seems to this group that this law opens serious questions as to the protection of heritage. And unfortunately, after the work done in the paper and after the debate in committee, we found that precisely the amendments that sought to correct the most serious aspects of this bill were not accepted. They only accepted those amendments that improved certain aspects of the law, but did not involve significant changes to the text, and especially those parts that mark heritage protection more significantly. Unfortunately, some amendments to Article 122 have been accepted, although not all, and at least some have been corrected, in order to expand the participation of wealth-related professionals, although we are waiting, as the BNG spokesman said, for a final transaction. Although we regret, in addition, that many of the amendments tabled by our group have fallen into a broken bag, because at least they would serve to avoid many of the evases that we understand has this bill and that they have to do with areas of deprotection for our heritage. As for the relationship with the Catholic Church, we have already stated that, in our view, a privileged relationship is established by maintaining a joint commission between the Council of Galicia and the Catholic Church in article 6, for which we present an amendment of suppression, since we believe this relationship of privilege to be unjustified, which also leaves for subsequent regulation of its functions. In addition, article 7 of the law establishes which are the advisory bodies in the field of cultural heritage, and it already includes the Catholic Church among others, so we do not agree with this privilege, since the actions in the properties of the Church are reported through a special commission while the rest of the people and administrations share the same organ. And another one of the evidences that we seem to be hard to understand in this bill is participation, because this law, in our opinion, also reduces participation in society. And in particular, the advisory and advisory bodies collected in article 7 with regard to the current law are reduced, assuming a fall in this sense in managing the heritage. That is why we introduced amendments that corrected this setback and, although the references to the recovery of the Technical Committee on Archaeology and Ethnography were accepted, we do not understand why we did not recover from the Museums too, as it was already collected in the current law, nor do we understand why we did not accept the amendments concerning the recovery of the Galician Culture Council, which this bill excludes as a consultative body; nor did they accept the recovery of the other real academies of Galicia linked to the Heritage Law and which are contained in the applicable law. One of the latest news announced by the council was to recast into a single law the existing legislation on the Ways of Santiago, which means simplification and staffing of it, something that this group seems to be positive. Here, too, we have to point out that, although one of the only two amendments of the People's Party to this law is the one that calls for the inclusion of the Portuguese Way along the coast – with which we agree – we must regret that the amendment concerning the state of abandonment and the failure to signal the Winter Way was not accepted, in which a concrete commitment was requested in an additional willingness to do so in six months, something that they have made deaf ears once again. And I take the opportunity to greet the members of the Association Friends of the Way of Santiago by Valdeorras, who are in the Chamber and who, unfortunately, find themselves in the lack of commitment by the Board to something so necessary and also as simple as to signal this path and take measures that put an end to the state of abandonment in which they are found; one shows more of the work that many people develop in an unselfish manner and which often eases the work of the Government of the Union to ensure the preservation of their heritage. If we have to celebrate that it seems that we can finally reach an agreement on an amendment tabled by the Socialist Group to include recognition as the Road of the Road of Translation, the Route of the Arousal Sea and the Ulla River, which, incomprehensiblely, was removed from the bill, when it was in the preliminary draft and also in the present law, as well as in the Plan Director of the Galician Board on the Ways of Santiago last year. Thanks to the pressure of the councils involved, I think we can get some support, because leaving this path out means something as surrealistic as not to grant Compostolá to the Apostle James himself. One of the main news presented by this bill is determined by legal authorization so that municipalities can authorize interventions in certain catalogued goods. It is important to note that it refers only to the goods that are included in the Catalog of Heritage, and that this group, which believes in municipalism as the way of management closest to the citizen, seems good to you that progress is being made in the involvement and autonomy of the municipalities to develop their policies, as is now the case in the historical assemblies with the approved special plans, in which they are already competent. However, we need to be critical of this paragraph in the sense that reality makes it very difficult to meet the objectives that mark this bill on this subject, since, as it is well known, most of the municipalities do not have enough resources or economic or human resources to be able to cope with these new functions and competences which since June are intended to give them, something which is curious coming from the People's Party, which, after this legislation, after the rules of Montoro, has reduced the local autonomy of the municipalities, which I have assumed the greatest backwardness of municipalism since democracy. So in this matter announced by law, it seems to us insufficient, because in order to make this effective, first of all, it is necessary to believe in local autonomy, which inevitably has to be accompanied by financial and human resources. The Board must allow technicians to be incorporated into the local administration, thus facilitating the management of this law to the municipalities. To this end it is necessary that, in addition to announcing this novelty, the Council of Galicia should assist the municipalities in hiring heritage management technicians and help financially, in order to develop this competition, as pointed out in Article 3 of this project, if not, everything remains in good intentions without any effectiveness or practical sense. Another of the main novelties that this bill establishes is the change in the categories of cultural heritage goods, from three to two categories. So the inventory disappears, leaving only the goods catalogued and the goods of cultural interest. And for this, the so-called Census of Cultural Heritage was created, defined not as a category of goods but as an instrument of disclosure of those goods that were not declared culturally or catalogued. And here's one of the points where the council clearly contradicted what it so much begged during the announcement of this bill, when it stated that this law did not entail any setbacks in the protection of Galician cultural heritage, because clearly this bill states that the goods included in the Census do not necessarily have to be physically preserved, when there is a cause of public interest. And even more so when it is claimed that the inclusion of a property in the Census will not determine the need for prior administrative authorization for interventions on the said good. For the Socialist Group this means an unfortunate step, with which we cannot agree at all, and it makes it clear that this bill opens a path to the deprotection of certain property assets, setting empty so that with discretion one can act on his own assets. The Socialist Group does not agree that property may be destroyed under any concept and that is why we are presenting amendments that substantially change this aspect and that we are concerned that they are not accepted. We propose that when there are causes of relevant public and social interest that make their conservation on-site incompatible, alternatives are established, such as the transfer of certain goods or their prior documentation, in the case of archaeological heritage, but legal insecurity, and even the door open to the disappearance of certain goods, we find it extremely worrying in a heritage law. And we also find it essential that the inclusion of a good in the Census of Cultural Heritage determine a protection based on the responsibility of the municipalities and the Council of Culture, and that you have to authorize any intervention affecting it. Another amendment that you didn't want to accept and that we can only regret. And in this sense, we've done fincapé in the protection of the goods included in the Census, also asking for its inclusion in article 35, which speaks of the protection of cultural heritage in urban planning. In view of their refusal to accept our proposals, we must state that they are categorically opening themselves up to the deprotection of certain cultural and heritage goods. And it should also be noted that this bill leaves open some issues that affect the protection of the heritage, because it is not well known what happens with the goods that are not included in the Census, for example, how it can be a mill, an alvarice, in the ethnographic field, or an archaeological deposit, which it is not known to be, by its very character, hidden, buried and hence unprotected. How are protection and conservation mechanisms established if the fact of being or not at the Census has no administrative and legal implications? These are issues that we believe were fundamental to solving for the effective protection of our heritage. Nor can we agree at all with the chronological criteria and dates that establish the law in several articles, such as the requirements for identifying certain elements as members of the protected heritage; a criterion that we cannot share because we believe that it can leave out of the protection multiple elements that must be part of our country's cultural heritage. And in this regard we also present several amendments, which were also rejected, which sought to establish other criteria other than chronological ones and which affected the importance and relevance of certain goods by matters more diverse than chronological ones. So, for all this, the Socialist Group cannot support this bill, because we consider, first, that it is unnecessary and, secondly, because in our view it constitutes a step backwards in the protection of our heritage. Nothing more and thank you very much.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u16	Thank you, Mr. Vázquez. Mr Baamnde Díaz has the floor for the People's Parliamentary Group.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u17	Thank you very much, Madam President. Greetings to your lordships; greeting also to all the people and representatives of associations who follow this debate. A debate on a heritage law which, as has already been said here, brings together the previous legislation on this subject: the 1995 Law of the Ways of Santiago, 1996 and also the 1992 Act of Artistic Staffing. Faced with the criticisms of the parliamentary opposition groups, from the People's Party, we defend the text of the paper, we defend the bill with energy and with consistency, because we understand, firstly, that it is a good law, a law that gives rise to the demands of wealth at the moment and a law that rests on three fundamental aspirations: simplification at three levels, at the legislative level, at the administrative level and at the level of organization; the incorporation of the best regulations and laws established both in the general legal framework and in the comparative right; and also the news of the right of international conventions. Even so, it is also intended to incorporate the planning tool to speed up management, give people greater security and the heritage and simplify administrative procedures. We also believe that, in view of what has been stated here, it is an absolutely necessary law. And it is a necessary law because the law must always be an expression of the social reality of the moment it must be applied, and in this sense it is true that the Law of 1995 deserves a positive assessment, but it is also true that the experience achieved in its application has allowed us to express itself in that application and in those circumstances of processing such as a great bureaucracy or slowness of processing or prejudice to citizenship, and many of these cases have been made manifest by the professionals and technicians of the Council of Culture, who have actively participated in the elaboration of the project and to whom I believe it should also be grateful for its work, as we have also extended this thanks to all those persons and associations to whom Mrs. Pontón referred to in her speech. But this new legislation is also necessary to bring it into line with the regulatory framework, the general framework, the changing international legislation on the subject, and also because it is obvious that there was a clear social demand for this legislative amendment, as the opinions expressed even in this Parliamentary House by numerous associations, entities and persons related to heritage and also the massive participation of social representation in the process of public claiming this Heritage Act. Obviously, the guiding principles of the law, its structure, have already been discussed in the debate of the whole, and the content, in the debate of the motion; therefore, allow me now to refer to the fundamental aspects of the law and to cause greater controversy in relation to the views expressed by the groups of the parliamentary opposition. First of all, we must stress that the law incorporates the new trends in the concept of heritage. Both in the exhibition of motives and in the articulate include the criterion of cultural identity of the Greek people, social cohesion and sustainable development. It develops the law the value of heritage in emotional, historical and territorial aspects and incorporates the evolutionary concept of heritage, its integrative character and the special typologies recognized internationally, specifically the international heritage. It collects the law the social concept of property, but a clarification to this expression: the law does not put the heritage under the market economy. The reference to the market economy is considered necessary to bring the constitutional requirements relating to that market economy into line with the law itself, and very especially to underline the increase in value that the safeguard of the heritage implies for economic operators, taking advantage of the value that has also the heritage, which also has the culture in general, as an element of economic and social dynamisation and also as a creator of wealth. Secondly, and as I said before, one of the objectives of the law is the establishment of instruments necessary to give more security to property and citizenship, as well as to speed up management. In particular, first of all, in order to manage assets, a reduction in the categories or types of assets is established, going from the three types before two, i.e. the BICs, to the catalogued goods. I make the clarification – so I will say – that, within this concept of catalogued goods, all those goods that were included at the entry into force of the heritage were included in the General Inventory of the Cultural Heritage of Galicia, except those that are declared BIC. Secondly, the catalogues ope legis, the catalogues individually and also those determined individually in the urban catalogues. As a result of this reduction, the design of the planning instrument is regulated, specifically the Galician Cultural Interest Goods Register and the Galician Cultural Heritage Catalogue. But especially it has to be mentioned that one of the important novelties of the law is the creation of the figure of the Census of Cultural Goods; a census that includes all those goods of the Greek cultural heritage in which it is appreciated that can compete with a cultural value but which is not yet declared or catalogued. Well, on this issue, I want to make reference to the fact that, by the parliamentary opposition, first of all, and with regard to the reduction of categories, criticism is made by claiming a deprotection and delagging of the heritage, precisely by the suppression of the Inventory. The rest of our respect must be expressed in our inconsistencies with that statement because it does not correspond to reality; firstly, because the Inventory does not disappear, the goods that have the consideration of BIC remain such that, and the rest of the goods become part of the Catalogue with the same level of protection. And secondly, the reduction of categories of goods does not imply any deprotection, but, quite the contrary, what it represents is simplicity and clarification. As far as the Census is concerned, there are also allegations, in the sense that it involves deprotection on the basis of two considerations: one, on the physical conservation of property, and another on the non-relationship, on the need for prior administrative authorization for interventions. I mean, we don't share this criterion either. It is not shared because Census is not a new category of goods, it is configured as an instrument of dissemination and promotion of heritage. Census is an additional tool for safeguarding the heritage, and its essential function is to recognize the existence of goods that have no protection, but that they may have consideration of heritage assets. But there is no de-protection, and there is no de-protection, first of all, because all those goods that must necessarily be preserved or those that are in danger or risk of disappearance must necessarily be declared or catalogued, and the other goods enjoy the protection provided for in Article 100 or in the additional first tenth provision. In this sense, I would like to stress that the Heritage Act which is subject to approval in no way represents a level of protection lower than the 1995 Act, on the contrary, increases this protection, moving forward in the identification of goods, in the classification of intervention typologies, establishing three levels of protection – integral protection, structural protection and environmental protection – and also defining the types of intervention. And even so, from the point of view of sustainable protection, this level of protection is enhanced by the establishment of protective contours and, in particular, of the buffer zone, which is another of the novelties that collects the law and does not generate deprotection, but exactly the opposite, is to strengthen that protection and ensure the display of the goods. It is not mandatory because it is not possible, and only in cases where it is expressed in a certain way. In any case, it meets international requirements in relation to the subject matter. Another important point of the law and also object of discussion is the fact that there is an administrative reorganization based on the increase in the participation and involvement of local administrations, municipalities. That's why legal empowerment is introduced as one of the main novelties so that municipalities can authorize interventions in certain cases and in certain types of goods, ensuring greater administrative agility and closer proximity to citizenship. He is pleased that this may involve a problem of competence and an honorable burden on the municipalities, if it is not accompanied by economic transfers. In this sense, we do not share that statement either, because we have to say, first of all, that there is no question of the competences of the Council of Galicia, because we are not talking about a problem of transfers, but about a problem and a question of collaboration. The project derives, not from functions and competences towards the municipalities, but from participation in procedures and processing, thus ensuring the homogeneity of criteria and administrative tools. Nor does it suppose an honorable burden for the municipalities, because the derivation of functions is not carried out ope lei for all the municipalities of Galicia. The subscription to this criterion of legal authorization, first of all, is voluntary, but secondly, it is guaranteed, because the municipalities that want to do so must meet special requirements, which are those that are determined in the additional fourth provision of the bill, and so even Junta also reserves in certain cases the granting of these authorizations and interventions in certain types of goods. Other positive aspects in relation to the law – which I quickly enumerate – are the new heritage typologies, intangible, industrial, underwater and scientific heritage. And by the way, Mr Ron, Article 9 incorporates definition and criteria of agreements for the safeguarding of UNESCO's intangible cultural heritage. Santiago's Paths are also regulated, which have been referred to here. The novelties in this project basically refer to aspects such as the BIC declaration of declared human heritage paths. The rest of the main routes are catalogued goods, the possibility of BIC's declaration at the request of the municipalities or the recognition of the Winter Road – and in this regard I want to underline the transcendence of that recognition. As to the criticism by the Socialist Parliamentary Group regarding delimitation, signaling or cleaning, it must be pointed out that logically delimitation and signaling must respond to the general criteria that apply to other paths, and regarding cleaning, ultimately, it is a particular issue that can be debated and that in some cases may be better or worse, but any provision must also be met in accordance with the general criteria. And I also want to underline – I was referring to Mr Vázquez – the transaction agreement to incorporate the Ways of Santiago the Route of Translation, by the Arousa Sea and the Ulla River, which leads not to a transitional provision, but to the additional provision tenth, and thus gives satisfaction to the demands made by an important part of people affected by that route. As for the sanctioning regime, I mean it's established under the highest security criterion, with more sanctionable conduct, but also with greater legal guarantees as well as avoiding defence. With regard to resources, it must be said that the law has sufficient financial resources for its implementation, and, in any case, regular budget allocation for the defence and conservation of property is not a matter of specific regulation in the law. With regard to the explanation of other amendments posed by opposition groups, we will give it in the second round. And it has to be said that we will ultimately support the text of the presentation, because it gives rise to a new legislative framework that responds to the current problems of heritage, but also to a need from a theoretical and conceptual point of view. From that point of view, we believe that we have advanced, because we protect a greater number of elements than in previous legislation, and establish a higher level of security for all operators, both with the formula of the two years of adaptation of special protection plans and with the publication of the Cultural Census of Heritage. And it's especially important to underline that you make sure...
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u18	Finish, please.
ParlaMint-ES-GA_2016-04-26-DSPG150.u19	...the dissemination of the cultural importance of the heritage through the timely public campaigns, and, Mrs. Martínez, also through the implementation of the knowledge of the heritage in the ESO itself. And at the same time, within the legal project, conceptual definitions are improved and uncertainty is avoided. Finally, I want to say that we are convinced that this law, which today is subject to debate and which is likely to be approved, constitutes a fundamental axis of all conservation, awareness-raising, heritage-relevant policy, with a regulation more adapted to current requirements and international requirements and to the experience accumulated in these years. That's why we're going to express our support for the text of the presentation. Nothing else and thank you very much.

Finally, the text of the Opinion is based on the explanatory statement; of 17 articles grouped into 4 chapters relating to the general provisions of the Galician social economy, to entities which integrate the participation of the sector and the creation of the Galician Social Economy Council, as well as to the promotion; of 5 additional provisions relating to coordination with the Galician Cooperative Council, with institutional participation, with the computerization of registers and electronic administration, with no increase in the budget allocations and with the establishment of the Welsh Plan for the encouragement of the activities of the social economy; of 2 transitional arrangements, the transitional regime applicable to the development of the Galician Social Services Act on consultative and participation bodies and the adaptation of administrative intervention to the market unit; of 1 single derogation provision and of three final amendments to the laws of cooperatives and social services, as well as to the authorisation for the recasting of the cooperatives, the provision of the right to enter into force of the title of the title of the title of the title of the title to be published in force of the Official Journal It is therefore necessary to complete the processing of this legislative procedure with the debate and vote on the amendments maintained and the Commission's ruling on the draft law on the social economy of Galicia which I have just tabled.
